Note: We must be careful with the root's parent. For root, parent[1] should be None or -1.

In the query, if v=1, then parent[1] is not defined.

So for a vertex v in query:
- if v == 1, then only 1 can be on the path (since parent[1] doesn't exist)
- so for v=1, we need 1 to be on the path

In our set S, we add parent[v] only if v != 1.

In the condition for v=1, we only need 1 to be in ancestors[x].

So in the algorithm, for each v in query, if v==1, then we only need 1 in ancestors[x].

For other v, we need v in ancestors[x] or parent[v] in ancestors[x].

We can modify the inner loop accordingly.

Let me code accordingly.

Steps in code:
1. Read n, m
2. Build adjacency list
3. DFS to compute parent and depth arrays
4. For each vertex, compute ancestors as a set (using parent chain)
5. For each query:
   - Create set S = set()
   - For each v in query:
        S.add(v)
        if v != 1:
            S.add(parent[v])
   - For each x in S:
        valid = True
        for each v in query:
            if v == 1:
                if 1 not in ancestors[x]:
                    valid = False
                    break
            else:
                if v in ancestors[x] or parent[v] in ancestors[x]:
                    continue
                else:
                    valid = False
                    break
        if valid:
            print("YES")
            break
   - else:
        print("NO")

This should work.

Now, let's code accordingly.

Note: We must be careful with parent of root.

In the DFS, we start from root=1, and when we build the tree, we ensure parent of 1 is not set.

In the code, for v=1, we only require that 1 is in the ancestors of x.

In the ancestors set, we include the vertex itself and all its ancestors.

We will compute ancestors using parent chain.

We can do a DFS from root to assign parent, and then for each vertex, we can build ancestors by walking up.

But to avoid TLE, we can build the ancestors set for each vertex using a while loop.

Since the depth is at most n, and we have n vertices, total work is O(n^2) in worst-case (chain tree, n=2e5, then n^2 = 4e10), which is too slow.

We need to build ancestors more efficiently.

We can do a DFS from root and for each vertex, store the ancestors in a set by including parent's ancestors and itself.

We can do: 
- ancestors[1] = {1}
- for each vertex v (except root), ancestors[v] = ancestors[parent[v]] | {v}

This is O(n) in total, because each vertex is added once.

Yes, this is correct.

So we can do:

 Initialize ancestors[1] = {1}
 For each vertex from 2 to n in increasing order of depth, or simply iterate by DFS order:

 We can do a DFS from root and for each child, set ancestors[child] = ancestors[parent] | {child}

 This will be O(n) total.

 Steps for precomputation:
 - Build parent array using DFS from root
 - ancestors[1] = {1}
 - Do a BFS or DFS in order (depth order) to set ancestors for each vertex: for each vertex v, ancestors[v] = ancestors[parent[v]] | {v}

 This is efficient.

 Let me do that.

 Implementation:

1. Read input
2. Build graph
3. DFS from root 1 to compute parent and depth
4. Use BFS/DFS to compute ancestors for each vertex (in topological order by depth)
5. For each query, as above

 Let's code accordingly.

 Note: We must ensure that the BFS/DFS is in increasing depth to avoid issues.

 But any order will work since ancestors[parent] is computed before.

 We can do BFS from root.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    ptr = 0
    n = int(data[ptr]); m = int(data[ptr+1]); ptr += 2
    
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    for i in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[ptr]); v = int(data[ptr+1]); ptr += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    # BFS from root 1
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    parent[1] = 0  # parent of root is 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Build ancestors for each vertex
    ancestors = [set() for _ in range(n+1)]
    # ancestors[1] = {1}
    ancestors[1] = {1}
    
    # Process vertices in increasing order of depth
    # We can do by BFS order or by depth
    order = []
    queue = deque([1])
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        order.append(u)
        for v in graph[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Process vertices in order of increasing depth (order from BFS)
    for u in order:
        if u == 1:
            continue
        ancestors[u] = ancestors[parent[u]].copy()
        ancestors[u].add(u)
    
    # Process queries
    output = []
    for _ in range(m):
        k = int(data[ptr]); ptr += 1
        query = []
        for i in range(k):
            query.append(int(data[ptr])); ptr += 1
        
        # Build set S: vertices in query and their parents (excluding root's parent)
        S = set()
        for v in query:
            S.add(v)
            if v != 1:
                S.add(parent[v])
        
        found = False
        for x in S:
            valid = True
            for v in query:
                if v == 1:
                    if 1 not in ancestors[x]:
                        valid = False
                        break
                else:
                    if v in ancestors[x] or parent[v] in ancestors[x]:
                        continue
                    else:
                        valid = False
                        break
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        output.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    print("\n".join(output))

solve()
```

Let me test with the sample.

The sample input has 6 queries.

I think it should work.

We'll submit this solution.

Note: The worst-case might be slow if the depth is large and queries are large, but the total sum of k_i is only 2e5, and in practice, the average k_i might be small.

Given the problem constraints, this should be acceptable.

I'll write the final code accordingly. 

One more thing: in the BFS for order, we might not need to do it, we can just use the DFS order, but the BFS order we did is fine.

Also, in the parent array, for root, parent[1]=0, and when v==1, we skip the parent.

In the set S, we only add parent[v] if v != 1.

In the condition for v=1, we only check if 1 is in ancestors[x].

This should be correct.

Let me run through the first example: [3,8,9,10]
- x=10: ancestors[10] = {1,3,7,8,9,10}
- v=3: in -> yes
- v=8: parent[8]=7 in -> 7 in ancestors[10]? yes -> yes
- v=9: in -> yes
- v=10: in -> yes -> YES

Correct.

I think this is the solution. 

Final code:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    ptr = 0
    n = int(data[ptr]); m = int(data[ptr+1]); ptr += 2
    
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[ptr]); v = int(data[ptr+1]); ptr += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    # BFS from root 1
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Build ancestors for each vertex
    ancestors = [set() for _ in range(n+1)]
    ancestors[1] = {1}
    
    # Get BFS order to process in level order
    order = []
    queue = deque([1])
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        order.append(u)
        for v in graph[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Process each vertex in BFS order
    for u in order:
        if u == 1:
            continue
        ancestors[u] = ancestors[parent[u]].copy()
        ancestors[u].add(u)
    
    # Process each query
    output = []
    for _ in range(m):
        k = int(data[ptr]); ptr += 1
        query = []
        for i in range(k):
            query.append(int(data[ptr])); ptr += 1
        
        # Create set S: vertices in query and their parents (only if not root)
        S = set()
        for v in query:
            S.add(v)
            if v != 1:
                S.add(parent[v])
        
        found = False
        for x in S:
            valid = True
            for v in query:
                if v == 1:
                    if 1 not in ancestors[x]:
                        valid = False
                        break
                else:
                    if v in ancestors[x] or parent[v] in ancestors[x]:
                        continue
                    else:
                        valid = False
                        break
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        output.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    print("\n".join(output))
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    ptr = 0
    n = int(data[ptr]); m = int(data[ptr+1]); ptr += 2
    
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[ptr]); v = int(data[ptr+1]); ptr += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    # BFS from root 1
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Build ancestors for each vertex
    ancestors = [set() for _ in range(n+1)]
    ancestors[1] = {1}
    
    # Get BFS order to process in level order
    order = []
    queue = deque([1])
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        order.append(u)
        for v in graph[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Process each vertex in BFS order
    for u in order:
        if u == 1:
            continue
        ancestors[u] = ancestors[parent[u]].copy()
        ancestors[u].add(u)
    
    # Process each query
    output = []
    for _ in range(m):
        k = int(data[ptr]); ptr += 1
        query = []
        for i in range(k):
            query.append(int(data[ptr])); ptr += 1
        
        # Create set S: vertices in query and their parents (only if not root)
        S = set()
        for v in query:
            S.add(v)
            if v != 1:
                S.add(parent[v])
        
        found = False
        for x in S:
            valid = True
            for v in query:
                if v == 1:
                    if 1 not in ancestors[x]:
                        valid = False
                        break
                else:
                    if v in ancestors[x] or parent[v] in ancestors[x]:
                        continue
                    else:
                        valid = False
                        break
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        output.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    print("\n".join(output))

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.


-----Example-----

1 4

3 7

9 10

3 2 4 6
3 2 1 5
3 4 8 2
2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO

The second query is $[2, 4, 6]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=2$. Then the vertex $4$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $6$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $2$ which belongs to this path.

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.

v=8: parent is 7, not in path (path is root only), so distance from 8 to path is 2 (8 to 7 to 1), so not good.

So only works if all query vertices are either root or have parent in path.

For path to root, only vertices with parent root are covered by distance 1.

So only works if all query vertices are children of root.

In the example, only 3 is child of root, 8,9,10 are not, so doesn't work.

So we need to try deeper u.

Since the query vertices are given, and we know that if a vertex v is not covered by its parent being on path, then v itself must be on path, so u must be in the subtree of v.

Therefore, for each query vertex v, if v is not covered by its parent, then v must be on the path, so u must be in the subtree of v.

This gives us a constraint that u must be in the subtree of v for each such v.

Therefore, u must be in the intersection of the subtrees of all v that are not covered by their parent.

But we still need to find if such u exists.

Given the complexity, and since the total sum of k_i is bounded, I'll try the following practical approach:

For each query:
1. Let candidates = set()
2. For each vertex in the query, add that vertex to candidates
3. For each vertex in the query, add each of its neighbors to candidates (parents and children)
4. For each candidate u in candidates, check if the path from root to u satisfies the condition
5. If any candidate works, answer is "YES", else "NO"

Since each query has at most k_i vertices, and each has at most 2 neighbors, the number of candidates is O(k_i), and k_i is bounded by n, and total sum of k_i is bounded, this should be efficient.

Let's implement this.

Steps:
- Build tree and store parent/children relationships
- Precompute depth and parent for each vertex
- For each query:
  - candidates = set()
  - for each v in query:
    - candidates.add(v)
    - for each neighbor w of v:
      - candidates.add(w)
  - for each u in candidates:
    - path_ancestors = ancestors of u (from root to u)
    - valid = True
    - for each v in query:
      - if v is in path_ancestors or any neighbor of v is in path_ancestors:
        - continue
      - else:
        - valid = False, break
    - if valid:
      - print "YES" and break
  - if no candidate works, print "NO"

This should work.

This matches the expected output.

So the solution is to:

1. Build tree and store adjacency list
2. For each vertex, store parent and depth
3. For each query, build candidate set of vertices to try as u
4. For each candidate u, check if all query vertices are either on the path from root to u or have a neighbor on the path
5. If any works, answer is "YES", else "NO"

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays using BFS from root (vertex 1)
    parent = [0] * (n + 1)
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    # BFS to compute parent and depth
    queue = deque([1])
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Get ancestors of a vertex u (in order from u to root)
    def get_ancestors(u):
        ancestors = set()
        curr = u
        while curr != 0:
            ancestors.add(curr)
            curr = parent[curr]
        return ancestors
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = line[0]
        query_vertices = line[1:k+1]
        
        # Build candidate set for u
        candidates = set()
        for v in query_vertices:
            candidates.add(v)
            # Add all neighbors of v
            for neighbor in adj[v]:
                candidates.add(neighbor)
        
        found = False
        for u in candidates:
            ancestors = get_ancestors(u)
            valid = True
            for v in query_vertices:
                # Check if v or any neighbor of v is in ancestors
                if v in ancestors or any(neighbor in ancestors for neighbor in adj[v]):
                    continue
                else:
                    valid = False
                    break
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        print("YES" if found else "NO")

solve()
```

import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays using BFS from root (vertex 1)
    parent = [0] * (n + 1)
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    # BFS to compute parent and depth
    queue = deque([1])
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Get ancestors of a vertex u (in order from u to root)
    def get_ancestors(u):
        ancestors = set()
        curr = u
        while curr != 0:
            ancestors.add(curr)
            curr = parent[curr]
        return ancestors
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.

9 10

2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.

I'll solve this step by step.

Looking at the problem, we need to determine if there exists a path from the root (vertex 1) to some vertex u such that each given vertex is either:
1. On the path from root to u, OR
2. Has distance 1 (adjacent) to some vertex on the path

Key insights:

1. If a vertex is not on the path, it must have a neighbor that is on the path.

2. Since we're dealing with a tree, there's a unique path from root to any vertex.

3. For any valid path from root to u, all vertices in our query set must either be on this path or be adjacent to some vertex on this path.

4. The path from root to u consists of vertices on the root-to-leaf path. 

5. If a vertex v is in our query set, then either:
   - v is on the path from root to u, OR 
   - one of v's neighbors is on the path from root to u.

6. Since the path goes from root to u, if a vertex's neighbor is on the path, that neighbor must be an ancestor of u (in the path from root to u).

7. For a vertex v, its valid neighbors are only those that lie on the path from root to u.

8. The key constraint is that all query vertices must be "covered" by the path.

Strategy:
- For each query, we need to check if there exists some vertex u such that all query vertices are either on the path from root to u or adjacent to some vertex on that path.

After reading the problem again, I realize that the answer is "YES" if and only if there exists a path from root to u such that each query vertex is either on the path or adjacent to the path.

Is that true?
Yes! In a tree, if a vertex v is not on the path from root to u, then for v to be adjacent to the path, one of its neighbors must be on the path. But the neighbors are: parent and children.

- The parent is a vertex on the path (since parent is ancestor of u) only if the path contains it.
- A child would be at a depth greater than v's depth, and if the path goes to u at depth d, then a child of v at depth hv+1 > hv would be at depth hv+1 > d if hv+1 > d, so it can't be on the path.

Therefore, v can only be adjacent to the path if its parent is on the path.

This is a critical observation!

So for each query vertex v:
- Either v is on the path (v is an ancestor of u), OR
- v's parent is on the path (v's parent is an ancestor of u).

In other words, the path must contain either v or its parent.

This means that for the query to be valid, there must exist a vertex u such that for every query vertex v, either:
- v is an ancestor of u, OR  
- parent(v) is an ancestor of u.

Since the path from root to u contains all ancestors of u, this is equivalent to saying that the path contains a "representative" for each query vertex: either the vertex itself or its parent.

Now, the path is a chain from root to u. So the set of ancestors of u is well-defined.

We need to check if there exists a vertex u such that for every query vertex v, either v or parent(v) is in the path from root to u.

This is equivalent to: the set of vertices {v or parent(v) for all v in query} must have a common "ancestor chain" from root to some u.

Actually, more precisely: let S be the set of vertices that are either in the query or are parents of vertices in the query.

We need to check if there exists a vertex u such that all vertices in S are ancestors of u.

But not exactly - for each query vertex v, we need either v or parent(v) to be an ancestor of u.

So we need that the set T = {v for each query v} ∪ {parent(v) for each query v} has all its elements as ancestors of some common u.

But since we're in a tree and all ancestors are on the path from root to u, if all these vertices are in the path from root to u, then the path must extend to at least the depth of the deepest vertex in T.

And since the path from root to u contains all ancestors of u, if we let u be the deepest vertex in the set T (by depth), then the path from root to u will contain all ancestors of u.

Therefore, the condition is that all vertices in T are ancestors of the vertex with maximum depth in T.

Wait, not quite. If a vertex in T has a deeper depth, then all others must have depth ≤ it.

But we need that each vertex in T is an ancestor of the deepest one in T.

Actually, in a tree, a vertex x is an ancestor of vertex y if and only if depth[x] ≤ depth[y] and the path from root to y passes through x.

But for any two vertices, one might not be ancestor of the other.

So the condition is that all vertices in T are on the path from root to some vertex u, which means they must all be in a single path from root to u.

This happens if and only if the maximum depth of vertices in T is reached by a single path, and all other vertices in T are on that path.

But in a tree, multiple vertices can be on a single path from root only if they are all on a chain from root to some leaf.

So the algorithm for a query:
1. For each query vertex v, add v and its parent to a set T.
2. Find the vertex in T with maximum depth.
3. Check if all other vertices in T are ancestors of this vertex.
4. If yes, answer is YES, otherwise NO.

But is that sufficient?

Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: query [3,8,9,10]
- v=3: add 3, parent(3)=1
- v=8: add 8, parent(8)=7  
- v=9: add 9, parent(9)=7
- v=10: add 10, parent(10)=9

T = {3,1,8,7,9,10}

Depths: 
- 1:0, 3:2, 8:3, 7:2, 9:3, 10:3

Max depth is 3 (vertices 8,9,10).

Is 8 an ancestor of 10? 8->7->9->10, yes.
Is 9 an ancestor of 10? 9->10, yes.
Is 3 an ancestor of 10? 3->1->7->9->10, yes.
Is 1 an ancestor of 10? yes.
Is 7 an ancestor of 10? yes.

All are ancestors of vertex 10 (depth 3). So YES.

Example 2: [2,4,6]
- v=2: add 2, parent(2)=1
- v=4: add 4, parent(4)=1
- v=6: add 6, parent(6)=2

T = {2,1,4,1,6} = {2,1,4,6}

Depths:
- 1:0, 2:1, 4:1, 6:2

Max depth is 2 (vertex 6).

Is 2 an ancestor of 6? Yes (path 1->2->6).
Is 4 an ancestor of 6? 4->1, but 1->2->6, so no direct connection. Is 4 an ancestor of 6? 4 and 6 have no common path from root to 6. The path from root to 6 is 1->2->6. Vertex 4 is at depth 1, child of 1, so path is 1->4. These paths don't merge at 6. So 4 is not an ancestor of 6.

So answer should be NO, but expected is YES.

This indicates a flaw in my reasoning.

- Vertex 2: on path
- Vertex 4: adjacent to 1 (which is on path) 
- Vertex 6: adjacent to 2 (which is on path)

So for vertex 4: parent is 1, which is on path.
For vertex 6: parent is 2, which is on path.
For vertex 2: is on path.

So the condition should be: for each query vertex v, either v or parent(v) is on the path.

In this case:
- v=2: v is on path (path is 1->2)
- v=4: parent(4)=1 is on path
- v=6: parent(6)=2 is on path

So the condition is satisfied.

The set T is {2,1,4,6}. We need that for each v in query, v or parent(v) is on the path from root to u.

For a given u, the path is from root to u, and we need that for each v, either v or parent(v) is on this path.

This is equivalent to saying that the set S = {v for v in query} ∪ {parent(v) for v in query} is a subset of ancestors of u.

But in the example, u=2, ancestors are {1,2}. 
- Is 4 in {1,2}? No. 
- Is 6 in {1,2}? No.

But for vertex 4, we don't need 4 to be on the path, we need parent(4)=1 to be on the path. Similarly for 6, we need parent(6)=2 to be on the path.

So the condition is that for each v, parent(v) is on the path OR v is on the path.

So we need that the set of vertices in the path from root to u includes either v or parent(v) for each v in the query.

In other words, the path must contain at least one of {v, parent(v)} for each v.

This is equivalent to: the path must contain a vertex from each pair (v, parent(v)).

The path is a chain from root to u. We need that for each query vertex v, the set {v, parent(v)} has at least one element that is on the path.

So the algorithm:
1. For each query vertex v, consider the pair {v, parent(v)}
2. We need to find a path from root to some u that contains at least one vertex from each pair.
3. This is equivalent to: find a vertex u such that the path from root to u contains at least one vertex from each pair.

Since the path contains all ancestors of u, we can check for each possible "target" vertex u whether the path from root to u contains at least one vertex from each pair.

But the number of possible u can be large.

However, note that if a path contains a vertex from a pair, and that vertex is not on the path, then it's not covered.

Important: if parent(v) is on the path, then v is covered. If v is on the path, then v is covered.

The only way a vertex v is not covered is if both v and parent(v) are not on the path.

But parent(v) might not be on the path even if v is not.

Since we're going from root, the path contains root. 

For a given u, the path is the ancestors of u.

So we need to check if there exists u such that for every query vertex v, either v is an ancestor of u or parent(v) is an ancestor of u.

We can solve this by trying candidates. What candidates should we try?

The candidates for u are:
- The vertices that are in the query or are parents of vertices in the query (since if u is deeper than any of these, the path definitely extends to them).

Actually, u must be at depth at least as deep as the maximum depth of any v or parent(v) that is not covered.

But more simply, we can try every vertex that is in the set T = query ∪ {parent(v) for v in query}.

For each such vertex u in T, we check if the path from root to u covers all query vertices.

Since |T| ≤ 2*k_i, and sum of k_i is bounded, this should be acceptable.

Algorithm for a query:
1. Query vertices: v_list
2. Build set T = v_list ∪ {parent(v) for v in v_list}
3. For each u in T:
   a. Let path be all ancestors of u (from root to u)
   b. For each query vertex v, check if v is in path or parent(v) is in path
   c. If yes for all v, return "YES"
4. If no such u found, return "NO"

But is this correct?

Let's test with example 2: query [2,4,6]
T = {2,4,6} ∪ {parent(2)=1, parent(4)=1, parent(6)=2} = {1,2,4,6}

- v=4: 4 in path? no. parent(4)=1 in path? yes → covered

This matches.

Therefore, the solution is:

1. Build tree and compute parent and depth using DFS from root (vertex 1)
2. For each query:
   a. Build set T = query vertices ∪ {parent of each query vertex}
   b. For each u in T:
        i. Get path from root to u (all ancestors)
        ii. For each query vertex v, check if v is in path or parent(v) is in path
        iii. If all are covered, return "YES"
   c. If no u in T works, return "NO"

Note: We can optimize by not checking u that are not on the path from root to any candidate. But the set T is already bounded by 2*k_i.

Since the total sum of k_i is ≤ 2*10^5, and each query has at most this, the total work over all queries is bounded.

But worst-case one query with k_i=10^5, then |T| = 2*10^5, and checking each u might take O(k_i) per u, so O(k_i^2) = 10^10, which is too slow.

We need to optimize.

Optimization:
- Instead of checking every u in T, we can note that if a path from root to u covers all query vertices, then u must be at depth at least as large as the maximum depth of any query vertex or any parent.
- But more importantly, we can use the fact that the path from root to u must contain a vertex from each pair (v, parent(v)).

We can try to find the minimum depth u such that for each v, either v is on path or parent(v) is on path.

Actually, we can consider that the path must include at least one vertex from each pair (v, parent(v)).

This is equivalent to: the path must contain a vertex from each of these pairs.

In trees, if we have a set of requirements (each requiring a vertex from a pair), then we can find if there's a path that contains at least one from each pair.

We can try to find the minimum depth where this happens.

But even better: we can note that the path from root to u covers all vertices up to depth d. 

For each pair (v, parent(v)), we can precompute the minimum depth such that at least one of v or parent(v) is reached.

But the only thing that matters is whether parent(v) is reached, or v is reached.

Actually, if we let d be the depth of u, then the path covers all ancestors up to depth d.

For a query vertex v:
- If depth[v] ≤ d, then v might be on the path (if v is on the path), or if parent(v) is on the path.
- But we need to ensure that either v is on the path or parent(v) is on the path.

But parent(v) is on the path if depth[parent(v)] ≤ d and parent(v) is on the path.

Since the path contains all ancestors up to depth d, parent(v) is on the path if depth[parent(v)] ≤ d.

Similarly, v is on the path if depth[v] ≤ d.

But this is not sufficient - we need that at least one of v or parent(v) is in the set of path vertices.

Actually, in the path to a vertex at depth d, it contains all vertices with depth from 0 to d that are on the path.

For a vertex with depth > d, it cannot be on the path.

For a vertex with depth ≤ d, it is on the path only if it is in the path from root to u at depth d.

But the path from root to u at depth d may not include a vertex at depth ≤ d if it's not on the root-to-u path.

For example, if u is at depth 2, path might be 1->2->3, then vertices at depth 1: only 2 is in path.

So we cannot just use depth as a condition.

But we can try: for a given depth d, we need that for each query vertex v, either:
- v is on the path to some u at depth d, or
- parent(v) is on the path to some u at depth d.

But it's getting complex.

Given the constraints and that the total sum of k_i is only 2*10^5, and that in practice the number of unique vertices in T per query is not too large, and that in many cases the path might be found quickly, we might be able to get away with the naive approach.

But worst-case could be bad.

Better approach:
- For each query, we only need to consider u that are in the set T.
- The size of T is at most 2*k_i.
- For each u in T, checking if all query vertices are covered takes O(k_i).
- So total per query: O(k_i^2).
- Sum over queries of k_i^2 might be large.

For example, if one query has k_i=1000, then k_i^2 = 10^6, and if there are many such queries, it could be bad.

But the sum of k_i is only 2*10^5, so the maximum k_i could be 2*10^5, and then k_i^2 = 4e10, which is too slow.

We need a better approach.

Optimization insight:
- We can try to find a single vertex u that works, and we might find it in one of the candidates.
- But still, worst-case might be bad.

Another insight: the path from root to u will cover all ancestors of u. 
- For a query vertex v, it is covered if either v is an ancestor of u or parent(v) is an ancestor of u.
- parent(v) is an ancestor of u if and only if parent(v) is on the path from root to u.

Since the path from root to u contains all ancestors of u, we can say that v is covered if either v is in the path or parent(v) is in the path.

To avoid checking all u in T, we can note that if a vertex u works, then u must be at depth at least max_depth among the vertices in the query or their parents.

Let d_max be the maximum depth among all vertices in T.

Then we only need to try u in T with depth >= some value.

But still, we might need to try many.

For safety, we can try only the vertices in T that are not "excessively deep".

Actually, we can try only the vertices in T that have depth >= max_depth of any query vertex in the query.

But still.

Another idea: the only vertices that work are those that have the maximum depth among all in T.

But example 2 shows that u=2 (depth 1) works, while max depth in T is 2 (vertex 6).

So not.

I think for now, I'll go with the straightforward approach, with a small optimization: only try u in T that have depth >= the depth of the deepest query vertex.

Because if u has depth < depth of some query vertex v, then v cannot be on the path (since path vertices have depth <= depth of u), and parent(v) might be at depth at most depth(v)-1, which might be < depth of u, so parent(v) might not be on the path.

For v to be covered, if depth[v] > depth[u], then parent(v) must be on the path, so depth[parent(v)] <= depth[u].

But parent(v) has depth depth[v] - 1.

So if depth[v] > depth[u], then we need depth[v] - 1 <= depth[u], which means depth[u] >= depth[v] - 1.

So for a vertex v in the query with depth[v] > depth[u], we need depth[u] >= depth[v] - 1.

This means that for each v with depth[v] > depth[u], we need depth[u] >= depth[v] - 1.

Therefore, if we let d = depth[u], then we need d >= max{ depth[v] - 1 for all v in query with depth[v] > d }.

But this is complex.

Given the time, I'll implement the straightforward approach and hope it passes for the given constraints.

Final implementation:

1. Build tree and compute depth and parent using DFS from root.
2. For each query:
   a. Create set T of query vertices and their parents.
   b. For each u in T:
        i. Get the path from root to u (by storing ancestors)
        ii. For each query vertex v, check if v in path or parent(v) in path
        iii. If yes for all, return "YES"
   c. If no u found, return "NO"

We'll need to store parent and depth arrays.

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays using DFS from root (1)
    parent = [0] * (n + 1)
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    stack = [1]
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while stack:
        u = stack.pop()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                stack.append(v)
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):
        data = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = data[0]
        query_vertices = data[1:]
        
        # Build set T: query vertices and their parents
        T = set()
        for v in query_vertices:
            T.add(v)
            T.add(parent[v])
        
        found = False
        # Try each u in T
        for u in T:
            # Get path from root to u (ancestors)
            path = set()
            current = u
            while current != 0:
                path.add(current)
                current = parent[current]
            
            # Check if all query vertices are covered
            valid = True
            for v in query_vertices:
                # Check if v is in path or parent[v] is in path
                if v not in path and parent[v] not in path:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        print("YES" if found else "NO")

solve()
```

Wait, I see a potential issue: a vertex might have parent 0 if it's the root, but in our case, parent[1]=0, and we skip it.

In the path, when current=1, parent[1]=0, so it stops.

For a query vertex v=1, its parent is 0, which is not in the path. But 1 itself should be in the path.

The condition: v in path or parent[v] in path.
- parent[1] = 0, so we need 1 in path.

This is correct.

If v=1, then in the path from root to u, if u is at depth >=0, 1 is always in the path (since path includes root).

In the path building: we start from u and go to parent until 0.

For any u, path will include 1 (the root), so if v=1, then 1 is in path.

So it should be fine.

Let me test with a simple case: query with [1]
- T = {1, parent[1]=0} = {1,0}
- Try u=1: path = {1}
  - v=1: in path → covered → YES
- Try u=0: skip since 0 not in vertices? But we only have vertices from 1 to n.

In our T, we have vertices in 1..n. parent[v] might be 0, but we add it to T.

In the loop, we are iterating over T, which includes 0?

In our code, T adds parent[v] which could be 0.

But we should only consider u in the tree (1 to n), because path from root to 0 doesn't exist.

In the tree, vertices are 1 to n. So we should only consider u in [1, n].

So we need to filter T to only include vertices from 1 to n.

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays using DFS from root (1)
    parent = [0] * (n + 1)
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    stack = [1]
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while stack:
        u = stack.pop()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                stack.append(v)
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):
        data = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = data[0]
        query_vertices = data[1:]
        
        # Build set T: query vertices and their parents (filter to 1..n)
        T = set()
        for v in query_vertices:
            T.add(v)
            p = parent[v]
            if p != 0:  # only add if not root's parent (0)
                T.add(p)
        
        # If the query includes vertex 1, its parent is 0, which we don't add
        # But 1 itself is added to T
        
        found = False
        # Try each u in T
        for u in T:
            # Get path from root to u (ancestors)
            path = set()
            current = u
            while current != 0:
                path.add(current)
                current = parent[current]
            
            # Check if all query vertices are covered
            valid = True
            for v in query_vertices:
                # Check if v is in path or parent[v] is in path
                if v not in path and parent[v] not in path:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        print("YES" if found else "NO")

solve()
```

But what about the case where parent[v] is not in path? For v=1, parent[1]=0, which is not in path (since path only has vertices >=1), so we need v=1 to be in path.

And v=1 will be in path if u is at depth >=0, which it always is.

Also, if a query has only vertex 1, then T = {1}, path for u=1 is {1}, and v=1 in path → covered.

What if query has only vertex 2? 
- T = {2, parent[2]} 
- If parent[2]=1, then T = {2,1}
- Try u=1: path={1} → v=2: is 2 in path? no, parent[2]=1 in path? yes → covered → YES
- Try u=2: path={1,2} → v=2 in path → covered → YES

So it works.

I think this should be correct.

Final code:

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays using DFS from root (1)
    parent = [0] * (n + 1)
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    stack = [1]
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while stack:
        u = stack.pop()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                stack.append(v)
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):
        data = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = data[0]
        query_vertices = data[1:]
        
        # Build set T: query vertices and their parents (only vertices 1 to n)
        T = set()
        for v in query_vertices:
            T.add(v)
            p = parent[v]
            if p != 0:  # avoid adding 0 (root's parent)
                T.add(p)
        
        found = False
        # Try each u in T
        for u in T:
            # Get path from root to u (ancestors)
            path = set()
            current = u
            while current != 0:
                path.add(current)
                current = parent[current]
            
            # Check if all query vertices are covered
            valid = True
            for v in query_vertices:
                # Check if v is on the path or parent of v is on the path
                if v not in path and parent[v] not in path:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        print("YES" if found else "NO")

solve()
```

import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Build parent and depth arrays using DFS from root (1)
    parent = [0] * (n + 1)
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    stack = [1]
    parent[1] = 0
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while stack:
        u = stack.pop()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent[u]:
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                stack.append(v)
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):
        data = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = data[0]
        query_vertices = data[1:]
        
        # Build set T: query vertices and their parents (only vertices 1 to n)
        T = set()
        for v in query_vertices:
            T.add(v)
            p = parent[v]
            if p != 0:  # avoid adding 0 (root's parent)
                T.add(p)
        
        found = False
        # Try each u in T
        for u in T:
            # Get path from root to u (ancestors)
            path = set()
            current = u
            while current != 0:
                path.add(current)
                current = parent[current]
            
            # Check if all query vertices are covered
            valid = True
            for v in query_vertices:
                # Check if v is on the path or parent of v is on the path
                if v not in path and parent[v] not in path:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        print("YES" if found else "NO")

solve()

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.

YES
YES
NO
NO

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a rooted tree with n vertices, root is vertex 1
2. For each query with k vertices, we need to determine if there exists a path from root to some vertex u such that:
   - Each query vertex either:
     - Belongs to the path from root to u, OR
     - Has distance 1 (is adjacent) to some vertex on that path

## Key Insights

1. If a vertex v is in the query, then either:
   - v is on the path from root to u, OR
   - v is a direct neighbor of some vertex on the path from root to u

2. For a vertex v to be "covered" by a path from root to u:
   - v must be on the path, or
   - v must be a child of some vertex on the path (since we can only have distance 1)

3. This means that if we have a path from root to u, all query vertices must either:
   - Be on this path, or

- v is on the path from root to u, or
- parent[v] is on the path from root to u

To check if p is a descendant of p0:
- We can do: while p != root and depth[p] > depth[p0]: p = parent[p]
- then if p == p0, then p0 is an ancestor of p, so p is a descendant of p0.

Wait, no: if we do that, we are going up from p, and if we reach p0, then p0 is an ancestor of p, so p is a descendant of p0.

Yes.

So the algorithm is:

- Precompute parent and depth arrays with BFS from root.
- For each query, collect parents of non-root query vertices.
- If empty, YES.
- Else, pick one parent p0.
- For each parent p in parents:
   - while p != root and depth[p] > depth[p0]: p = parent[p]
   - if p != p0: then p is not a descendant of p0 -> return "NO"
- If all are descendants, return "YES"

However, we need to be careful: if p0 is not the root, and we go up from p, we might reach a node that is not p0.

But the condition is that p should be a descendant of p0, which means that in the path from root to p, it must pass through p0.

So we can do:

def is_descendant(a, b):  # a is the ancestor, b is the descendant
    while b != root and depth[b] > depth[a]:
        b = parent[b]
    return b == a

But in our case, we have a and b, and we want to know if a is an ancestor of b.

So function: is_ancestor(a, b) -> whether a is on the path from root to b

We can implement it as:

def is_ancestor(a, b):
    while b != 1 and depth[b] > depth[a]:
        b = parent[b]
    return b == a

Actually, b should be set to parent[b] until it reaches the level of a or goes beyond.

But we need to ensure that a is on the path.

We can do:

 while depth[b] > depth[a]:
     b = parent[b]
 if b == a:
     return True
 else:
     return False

But what if a is not on the path? After reducing depth[b] to depth[a], if b becomes a, then a is on the path.

Yes.

So the final algorithm:

1. Preprocess: build tree, then do BFS/DFS from root to compute parent and depth for each node.
2. For each query:
   - parents_set = set()
   - for each vertex v in query:
        if v != 1: parents_set.add(parent[v])
   - if parents_set is empty: print "YES"
   - else:
        p0 = next(iter(parents_set))
        valid = True
        for p in parents_set:
            if not is_ancestor(p0, p):  # p0 is an ancestor of p
                valid = False
                break
        print("YES" if valid else "NO")

Why didn't our algorithm work? Because in our algorithm, we required that all parents are on the path, but in this case, we don't need to have all parents on the path; we only need that for each vertex, either the vertex or its parent is on the path.

In the algorithm above, we only got that the parents are on the path, but actually, for vertex 5, we only need parent 2 to be on the path, not necessarily that 5 is on the path.

So our condition was too strong.

We only require that for each vertex v in query (v != 1), either v is on the path or parent[v] is on the path.

This means that the path from root to u must contain either v or parent[v].

For a set of vertices, we need to check if there exists a path from root to u such that for each v, either v or parent[v] is on the path.

This is equivalent to: the path must contain the union of {v, parent[v]} for each v in query.

But since v may not be on the path, and parent[v] may be on the path.

But actually, since the path is from root to u, and contains a set of vertices, we need that for each v, either v or parent[v] is in the path.

This is the same as: the path from root to u must contain at least one of v or parent[v] for each v.

This is a covering condition.

We can reformulate: let S be the set of query vertices.

For each v in S, we have two options: include v in the path or include parent[v] in the path.

We need to find if there exists a path from root to u that contains at least one vertex from each of the pairs {v, parent[v]}.

This is a set cover with one vertex per pair.

But we can do better.

Note that if v is the root, then parent[v] is not defined, and v is on every path, so it's covered automatically.

For non-root vertices, we need to cover each with either itself or its parent.

The path from root to u is a chain. So we need to choose a chain from root to u that covers all the requirements.

This is equivalent to: the chain must contain at least one vertex from each pair {v, parent[v]}.

We can try to find the LCA of all requirements.

Known solution from similar problems: the answer is "YES" if and only if the following holds:
- For every vertex v in the query that is not the root, either v is on a path from root to some u, or parent[v] is on a path from root to some u.

But since the path is fixed, and we can choose u, we need to see if the set of vertices {v or parent[v]} for each v in query can be covered by a single path from root to u.

This is equivalent to: the set of vertices that need to be covered (each requiring either itself or its parent) must be contained in some path from root to u.

In other words, there exists a vertex u such that the path from root to u contains at least one vertex from each pair {v, parent[v]}.

This is a classic problem that can be solved by:  
- For each vertex in the query, the requirement is that either the vertex or its parent is on the path.
- This is equivalent to: the path from root to u must contain at only one vertex from each pair.

We can think of it as: the path must avoid being in a position where for some v, both v and parent[v] are not on the path.

To solve it, one known approach is to take the set of all vertices that are in the query or are parents of query vertices.

Then, the path from root to u must contain at least one of v or parent[v] for each v.

This is equivalent to: the path from root to u must be such that it intersects every pair {v, parent[v]}.

In a tree, this is always possible unless the pairs are in different branches that cannot be covered.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.


-----Example-----
Input
10 6
1 2
1 3
1 4
2 5
2 6
3 7
7 8
7 9
9 10
4 3 8 9 10
3 2 4 6
3 2 1 5
3 4 8 2
2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO



-----Note-----

The picture corresponding to the example:

[Image]

Consider the queries.

The first query is $[3, 8, 9, 10]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path from the root $1$ to the vertex $u=10$. Then vertices $[3, 9, 10]$ belong to the path from $1$ to $10$ and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which also belongs to this path.

The second query is $[2, 4, 6]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=2$. Then the vertex $4$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $6$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $2$ which belongs to this path.

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.

The fifth and the sixth queries both have answer "NO" because you cannot choose suitable vertex $u$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ## Problem Understanding

1. We have a rooted tree with n vertices, root is vertex 1
2. For each query with k vertices, we need to determine if there exists a path from root to some vertex u such that:
   - Each query vertex either belongs to the path from root to u, OR
   - Each query vertex has distance exactly 1 to some vertex on the path

## Key Insights

1. If a vertex v is in the query, then either:
   - v is on the path from root to u (meaning v is an ancestor of u), OR
   - v is adjacent to some vertex on the path (meaning v has a neighbor on the path)

2. For a vertex to be "covered" by a path from root to u, it must be either:
   - An ancestor of u, or
   - Have a neighbor that is an ancestor of u

3. The path from root to u is uniquely determined by u.

4. For a valid solution to exist, all vertices in the query must be "near" the path (distance ≤ 1).

- All parents of vertices in the query
- All children of vertices in the query, but only those at depth ≥ max_depth of query vertices? 

Actually, for a query vertex v to be covered if u is a child of v, then v must be on the path, so v is ancestor of u, which means u is in v's subtree.

So candidates for u are vertices that are either:
- In the query, or
- The parent of some query vertex, or
- The child of some query vertex

The number of such vertices is at most 3 * k per query.

Total sum of k_i is 2*10^5, so total work is O(sum of k_i^2) = O(4*10^10) which is too much.

We need a linear or near-linear per query.

Another approach: for the entire query to be coverable, for each vertex v in the query, either:
- v is an ancestor of the path to u, or 
- one of v's neighbors is an ancestor of u.

A necessary and sufficient condition might be that the set of ancestors that cover the query vertices form a chain from root to u.

I found a known solution for this problem in contests: 

The answer is "NO" if and only if there exists a vertex v in the query such that v has no parent in the set of vertices that are ancestors of any query vertex or have a child in the query that is on the path.

After careful thought, I believe the intended solution is:

1. For each query, collect the set of query vertices.
2. For each query vertex v, the set of its "covering" nodes are:
   - v itself (if v is on path)
   - v's parent (if parent is on path)
   - any child of v (if child is on path)

3. We need to find if there exists a path from root to u such that for each v, there is a covering node on the path.

This is equivalent to: does there exist a vertex u such that the path from root to u intersects the covering set of each v.

The covering set of v is {v, parent(v), children(v)}.

But only if those vertices are in the right subtree.

I think I need to implement the candidate approach with optimization.

Given the constraints on sum of k_i, and that the total number of vertices across queries is 2*10^5, I'll try the following:

For each query:
- Find the maximum depth among query vertices
- Consider candidates for u: all vertices in the query, and all parents of query vertices
- For each candidate u with depth >= max_depth, check if all query vertices are covered
- If any candidate works, answer is "YES", otherwise "NO"

The number of candidates per query is at most 2*k_i, and total work is sum over queries of (2*k_i * k_i) = 2 * sum(k_i^2)

In the worst case, if one query has k=200000, then sum(k_i^2) could be huge.

But it's guaranteed that sum of k_i ≤ 2*10^5, so the maximum k_i is at most 2*10^5, and the sum of squares could be large.

For example, if one query has k=200000, then k^2 = 4e10, which is too much.

We need a better approach.

Final insight from known problems: the answer is "NO" if and only if there exists a vertex v in the query such that:
- v is not the root, and 
- v's parent is not in the query, and 
- v has no child in the query, and 
- v is not on any path that can cover it

Actually, after more thought, I found that in such problems, the condition is related to the LCA.

I think I should implement the candidate approach and hope that in practice, the number of candidates is small or the constraints are such that it's acceptable.

Alternatively, a known solution is to use the following:

The path exists if and only if the following holds: for every vertex v in the query, either:
- v is on the path, or 
- v's parent is on the path, or 
- v's child is on the path

But this is for a fixed path, not for existence.

I think I need to output a solution. Based on the examples and common patterns, I'll implement the candidate approach with the following candidates for u:

Candidates for u:
- The vertex in the query with maximum depth
- Each vertex in the query
- Each parent of a vertex in the query

And for each candidate, check if it works.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # BFS to compute parent and depth from root 1
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Function to check if a vertex u can cover all query vertices
    def can_cover(query_vertices, u):
        # Check if all query vertices are covered by path from root to u
        path_set = set()
        curr = u
        while curr != 0:
            path_set.add(curr)
            curr = parent[curr]
        
        for v in query_vertices:
            covered = False
            # v is covered if v is on the path from root to u
            if v in path_set:
                covered = True
            else:
                # v has distance 1 to some vertex on the path
                # check v's parent
                if parent[v] in path_set:
                    covered = True
                else:
                    # check v's children - but children are not on the path
                    # because path is from root to u, only ancestors are on path
                    # so only parent can be on path
                    # if no parent on path, then no neighbor on path
                    covered = False
            if not covered:
                return False
        return True
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):
        data = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = data[0]
        query_vertices = data[1:k+1]
        
        # Get candidates for u
        candidates = set()
        
        # Add vertices in query
        for v in query_vertices:
            candidates.add(v)
        
        # Add parents of vertices in query
        for v in query_vertices:
            if parent[v] != 0:
                candidates.add(parent[v])
        
        # Also add vertex with maximum depth among query vertices
        max_depth_v = query_vertices[0]
        for v in query_vertices:
            if depth[v] > depth[max_depth_v]:
                max_depth_v = v
        candidates.add(max_depth_v)
        
        # Also add the root if not in candidates
        if 1 not in candidates:
            candidates.add(1)
        
        found = False
        for u in candidates:
            if depth[u] < min(depth[v] for v in query_vertices):
                continue
            if can_cover(query_vertices, u):
                found = True
                break
        
        print("YES" if found else "NO")

solve()
```

Wait, there's a flaw. In the covering condition, for a vertex v, if v is not on path, then we need v's parent to be on path. But what if v has a child on the path? That child would be an ancestor of u, so the child is on the path. 

For example, if v has a child w, and w is on the path from root to u, then distance(v, w) = 1, so v is covered.

So we also need to check if any child of v is on the path.

So in the can_cover function, we should check:
- v is on path, or
- parent of v is on path, or
- any child of v is on path

But "any child of v is on path" means that there exists a child w of v such that w is on the path from root to u.

In the path from root to u, a vertex is on path if it is an ancestor of u.

So for a child w of v to be on path, v must be an ancestor of w, and w must be an ancestor of u.

So w must be in the path.

In the can_cover function, we should check if any child of v is in the path_set.

So let's modify:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # BFS to compute parent and depth from root 1
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Function to check if a vertex u can cover all query vertices
    def can_cover(query_vertices, u):
        # Get the path from root to u
        path_set = set()
        curr = u
        while curr != 0:
            path_set.add(curr)
            curr = parent[curr]
        
        for v in query_vertices:
            covered = False
            # v is covered if v is on the path from root to u
            if v in path_set:
                covered = True
            else:
                # v is covered if v's parent is on the path
                if parent[v] in path_set:
                    covered = True
                else:
                    # v is covered if any child of v is on the path
                    # Check if any child of v is in path_set
                    # Get children of v (in the tree)
                    children_set = set()
                    for child in graph[v]:
                        if parent[child] == v:
                            children_set.add(child)
                    for child in children_set:
                        if child in path_set:
                            covered = True
                            break
            if not covered:
                return False
        return True
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(m):
        data = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = data[0]
        query_vertices = data[1:k+1]
        
        # Get candidates for u
        candidates = set()
        
        # Add vertices in query
        for v in query_vertices:
            candidates.add(v)
        
        # Add parents of vertices in query
        for v in query_vertices:
            if parent[v] != 0:
                candidates.add(parent[v])
        
        # Also add vertex with maximum depth among query vertices
        max_depth_v = query_vertices[0]
        for v in query_vertices:
            if depth[v] > depth[max_depth_v]:
                max_depth_v = v
        candidates.add(max_depth_v)
        
        # Also add the root if not in candidates
        if 1 not in candidates:
            candidates.add(1)
        
        found = False
        for u in candidates:
            # Ensure that the path to u covers at least the minimum depth
            if depth[u] < min(depth[v] for v in query_vertices):
                continue
            if can_cover(query_vertices, u):
                found = True
                break
        
        print("YES" if found else "NO")

solve()
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # BFS to compute parent and depth from root 1
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)

    visited[1] = True
    depth[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Function to check if a vertex u can cover all query vertices
    def can_cover(query_vertices, u):

            curr = parent[curr]

                covered = True
            else:

                    covered = True
                else:

                    # Find children of v
```

        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.


-----Example-----
Input

1 2

1 4
2 5
2 6
3 7
7 8
7 9
9 10
4 3 8 9 10
3 2 4 6
3 2 1 5
3 4 8 2
2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO

The second query is $[2, 4, 6]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=2$. Then the vertex $4$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $6$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $2$ which belongs to this path.

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a rooted tree with n vertices, root is vertex 1
2. For each query, we're given k distinct vertices
3. We need to determine if there exists a path from root (vertex 1) to some vertex u such that:
   - Each given vertex either:
     - Belongs to the path from root to u, OR
     - Has distance 1 (is adjacent) to some vertex on that path

## Key Insights

1. If a vertex is on the path from root to u, it must be an ancestor of u (including u itself)
2. If a vertex has distance 1 to the path, it must be adjacent to some vertex on the path
3. For a vertex to be "covered" by the path from root to u, it either:
   - Lies on the path (is an ancestor of u), OR
   - Is a direct child of some vertex on the path

## Strategy

For a given query, I need to check if there exists some vertex u such that all query vertices are either:
- Ancestors of u, OR
- Children of some ancestor of u

The key observation: if a vertex v is part of the query, then either:
- v is on the path from root to u (so v is an ancestor of u), OR
- v is adjacent to some vertex on the path (so v is a child of some ancestor of u)

This means that for each query vertex, we need to check its parent and all its ancestors.

## Algorithm

1. Build the tree and compute parent-child relationships
2. For each query, try to find a valid u:
   - The path from root to u must contain all the vertices that have no alternative
   - A vertex can "cover" itself if it's on the path, or its parent if the parent is on the path
   - We need to find a common path that "covers" all query vertices
